== privatelink-provider-nlb-billed-hourly-full-hour | pricing-dimension | hard=True | rel=core | vol=stable | ch=- | b=4.4.S3 | note=Grounds the provider-side fixed cost of a PrivateLink endpoint service (Callvale d11-101); the endpoint itself is billed | st=mech-pass rev=3 | by=mine-11-behavior+decisions

== transit-gateway-fca-allocation-options-default-source | default | hard=True | rel=core | vol=changed-since-2022 | ch={"date": "2025-11-21", "was": "Transit Gateway only used a sender-pay model", "was_evidence": 3} | b=4.4.S3 | note=Older exam questions assume the sender always pays. Checked against the metering-policy page and the What's New post. | st=mech-pass rev=1 | by=mine-11-numbers+changes
  C: By default a transit gateway allocates data processing charges to the account that owns the source attachment; since Flexible Cost Allocation (November 21, 2025) a metering policy can instead allocate charges to the destination attachment owner or the transit gateway owner.
  Q: Which accounts can a transit gateway metering policy charge for data processing, and which is the default?
  D[default] (6): account that owns the source attachment
  D[dest] (4): owns the destination attachment
  D[tgw] (4): owns the transit gateway
  S: transit gateway data processing charges | dom=default | adj=True | cond=a metering policy rule can name the source attachment owner, the destination attachment owner or the transit gateway owner
  E: docs/vpc/latest/tgw/metering-policy :: By default, transit gateway uses a sender-based cost allocation model where data processing charges are allocated to the account that owns the source attachment.
  E: docs/vpc/latest/tgw/metering-policy :: **Destination attachment owner** - Charges are allocated to the account that owns the destination attachment
  E: docs/vpc/latest/tgw/metering-policy :: **Transit Gateway owner** - Charges are allocated to the account that owns the transit gateway
  E: www/about-aws/whats-new/2025/11/aws-transit-gateway-flexible-cost-allocation :: Previously, Transit Gateway only used a sender-pay model, where the source attachment account owner was responsible for all data usage related costs.
  CI: ["docs/vpc/latest/tgw/doc-history#12", "whats-new-v2#launch-6xhxsc88takkvaeqaq3rk4"]

== transit-gateway-wp-traffic-stays-on-backbone | behavior | hard=True | rel=core | vol=stable | ch=- | b=3.4.K4 | note= | st=mech-pass rev=1 | by=mine-11-behavior+decisions

In a centralized approach, you will apply and manage policies for all spoke VPC access on a single endpoint. With growing number of VPCs, the complexity of maintaining least privilege with a single policy document might grow. Single policy document also results in larger

  E: www/vpc/faqs :: Inter-Region VPC Peering traffic goes over the AWS backbone that has in-built redundancy and dynamic bandwidth allocation.
  E: www/vpc/faqs :: If an Inter-Region peering connection does go down, the traffic will not be routed over the internet.

You must associate an IPv6 CIDR block with each VPC, enable the instances in the VPCs for IPv6 communication, and route IPv6 traffic intended for the peer VPC to the VPC peering connection.

== vpc-peering-no-transitive-example | restriction | hard=True | rel=core | vol=stable | ch=- | b=4.4.K6 | note= | st=mech-pass rev=1 | by=mine-11-behavior+decisions
  C: VPC peering does not support transitive peering: if VPC A peers with B and with C, traffic cannot go from B to C through A, and B and C need their own peering connection.
  Q: If VPC A is peered with VPC B and VPC C, can B reach C through A?
  D[v1] (6): does not support transitive peering relationships
  D[v2] (8): route traffic from VPC B to VPC C
  S: VPC peering connection | dom= | adj=False | cond=
  E: docs/vpc/latest/peering/vpc-peering-basics :: VPC peering does not support transitive peering relationships. For example, if there are VPC peering connections between VPC A and VPC B, and between VPC A and VPC C, you can't route traffic from VPC B to VPC C through VPC A. To route traffic between VPC B and VPC C, you must create a VPC peering connection between them.

== vpc-peering-one-to-one-relationship | behavior | hard=True | rel=core | vol=stable | ch=- | b=4.4.K6 | note= | st=mech-pass rev=2 | by=mine-11-behavior+decisions
  C: A VPC peering connection is a one to one relationship between two VPCs, and you have no peering relationship with VPCs that your VPC is not directly peered with.
  Q: What kind of relationship is a VPC peering connection?
  D[v1] (8): a one to one relationship between two VPCs
  D[v2] (4): not directly peered with
  S: VPC peering connection | dom= | adj=False | cond=

  C: In the many-to-many pattern traffic between each pair of VPCs is managed individually; in the hub-and-spoke pattern all inter-VPC traffic flows through a central resource that routes it by established rules.
  Q: What is the difference between many-to-many and hub-and-spoke VPC connectivity?
  D[v1] (2): many-to-many approach
  D[v2] (8): all inter-VPC traffic flows through a central resource

  E: docs/whitepapers/latest/building-scalable-secure-multi-vpc-network-infrastructure/vpc-to-vpc-connectivity :: In the many-to-many approach, the traffic between each VPC is managed individually between each VPC. In the hub-and-spoke model, all inter-VPC traffic flows through a central resource, which routes traffic based on established rules.

== vpc-peering-wp-use-non-overlapping-cidr-per-vpc | behavior | hard=False | rel=core | vol=stable | ch=- | b=3.4.S1 | note= | st=mech-pass rev=1 | by=mine-11-behavior+decisions
  C: AWS advises giving each VPC a single, contiguous, non-overlapping CIDR block, because VPC-to-VPC connectivity works best with non-overlapping IP ranges for the VPCs being connected.
  Q: What CIDR planning does AWS advise before connecting VPCs?
  D[v1] (8): non-overlapping IP ranges for each VPC being connected
  D[v2] (5): single, contiguous, non-overlapping CIDR block
  S: VPC-to-VPC connectivity | dom= | adj=False | cond=
  E: docs/whitepapers/latest/aws-vpc-connectivity-options/amazon-vpc-to-amazon-vpc-connectivity-options :: VPC connectivity between VPCs is best achieved when using non-overlapping IP ranges for each VPC being connected. For example, if you’d like to connect multiple VPCs, make sure each VPC is configured with unique Classless Inter-Domain Routing (CIDR) ranges. Therefore, we advise you to allocate a single, contiguous, non-overlapping CIDR block to be used by each VPC.
== vpc-peering-wp-use-tgw-or-cloud-wan-as-hub | behavior | hard=True | rel=core | vol=stable | ch=- | b=3.4.S1 | note= | st=mech-pass rev=2 | by=mine-11-behavior+decisions
  C: To let all VPCs talk to each other with VPC peering you need 1:1 peering connections between each pair; alternatively you can use AWS Transit Gateway or AWS Cloud WAN as a network transit hub.
  Q: What can replace a full mesh of 1:1 peering connections?
  D[v1] (9): create 1:1 VPC peering connections between each of them
  D[v2] (7): AWS Transit Gateway or AWS Cloud WAN
  S: VPC peering design | dom= | adj=False | cond=
  E: docs/whitepapers/latest/aws-vpc-connectivity-options/vpc-peering :: Amazon VPCs do not support transitive peering, meaning that you can’t communicate two VPCs that are not directly peered using a third VPC as transit. If you want all of your VPCs to communicate with each other using VPC peering, you will need to create 1:1 VPC peering connections between each of them. Alternatively, you can use AWS Transit Gateway or AWS Cloud WAN to act as a network transit hub.
